Did You Know? The Arizona Game and Fish Department is responsible for wildlife management
in the state and receives no state tax dollars. Hunters and anglers pay for wildlife conservation
through their purchase of licenses, tags and stamps, and excise taxes on hunting and fishing
gear. The conservation dollars paid by hunters and anglers benefit both game and nongame
species and their habitats.

2026-2027 ARIZONA HUNTING REGULATIONS 1
===== PAGE 5
Important Information for 2026-2027
This list is for informational purposes only and lists the major changes that have occurred since publication of the last
regulations booklet. Individuals should thoroughly read and understand the appropriate regulations prior to submitting an
application or going afield. If you have questions, please call 602-942-3000. Get more information at azgfd.gov.
Hunt permit application deadline is Tuesday, June 2, 2026, at 11:59 p.m. Arizona time. Applications must be submitted online
at azgfd.gov. Protect your investment by purchasing PointGuard (see page 7 for details).
Know Your Customer ID Customer IDs are required in the draw application. The Customer ID Retrieval Tool can be accessed
Protect your points for
at www.azgfd.gov/draw and during the draw application process to lookup the number. If the account is located, AZGFD
will send the Customer ID number to the associated email address. If you have done business with the Department and are
unable to retrieve your Customer ID, please call 602-942-3000.
$10 per species or $25 for
Get Draw Results Online with a Portal Account Dependent account features are available with a portal account, so everyone —
including youth — can view their draw results online as soon as they are posted. Create an account at www.azgfd.gov/register.
three consecutive draw cycles!
Keep Credit Card Information Current Applicants are encouraged to keep their credit card payment information current. If
your payment is declined at the time of the draw, your application will not be drawn. The Department will provide notice on its
website when the deadline to update credit cards is finalized. If your credit/debit card has been used for multiple applications,
notify your banking institution that multiple charges from Arizona Game and Fish could be processed simultaneously.
Mandatory Harvest Reporting Update Starting in fall 2026, mandatory harvest reporting requirements for bear, mountain
lion and over-the-counter archery deer will be accepted online only. Harvest limits have been set by unit and species for bear,
mountain lion and over-the-counter archery deer seasons. Hunters are required to report their harvest within 48 hours at
azgfd.gov\harvestreports; the call center and phone numbers will no longer be an eligible way to report your harvest.
Bag Limit Change for Deer, Javelina and Bear (Population Management Seasons Only) Periodically, the department offers
Population Management Hunts under the provisions of R12-4-115 (page 92). Participants for these hunts are selected via a
random drawing from the Supplemental Hunter Pool (page 49). The increased bag limit changes for deer, javelina and bear
only apply to animals harvested with a restricted nonpermit-tag issued through this process.
Sandhill Crane Mandatory Check Station All General sandhill crane hunts have a mandatory check station of harvested
sandhill cranes this year. Successfully drawn hunters will be sent additional information by email on how to comply.
Canyon Lake Hunter Access Canyon Lake will undergo a major drawdown starting Sept. 16, 2026, for approximately 16 weeks
to perform mandatory safety inspections and maintenance on the Mormon Flat Dam. The lake, including all boat ramps and
access points managed by the Tonto National Forest, will be closed to the public during this four-month period. A majority
of the huntable area in Unit 22 north of the lake for desert bighorn sheep and other game species will be restricted until the
maintenance is complete.
Crossbow and CHAMP Permits All existing crossbow permits and Challenged Hunter Access/Mobility Permits (CHAMPs)
will expire on June 30, 2026. Any person seeking to obtain a crossbow permit (R12-4-216) or CHAMP (R12-4-217) effective July
1, 2026, or after must complete a new medical evaluation and submit a new application to the department.
Hunter Harvest Questionnaire Your Hunter Harvest Questionnaire may come by mail, email, text, or all three methods.
Don’t miss your chance to protect your points!
Please take a moment to respond. A QR code, as well as a web link, is also located on the back of your tag. Scan the code to
access our Hunter Harvest Questionnaire webpage (you may need to download a QR reader app to scan the code). Completing
Add flexibility to your hunt and peace of mind
your hunter questionnaire honestly and accurately is important for management.
while providing funding for on-the-ground

Use of Trail Cameras Pursuant to Commission Rule R12-4-303.A.5, a person shall not use any images, video or data/
See page 7 for details. information from a trail camera for the purposes of taking or locating wildlife, or aiding in the take of wildlife. For the
purposes of this information, “take or taking” means hunting, pursuing, capturing or killing. Scouting prior to a hunt also falls
under the definition of take.
Prescribed Fire Prescribed fire is a tool used by the U.S. Forest Service, Bureau of Land Management (BLM) and other public
land managers to improve wildlife habitat and overall ecological health for public lands. Prescribed fires often occur during
hunting seasons, and it is common for hunters to see both fire and smoke. More information about planned and active fires
www.azgfd.gov/pointguard
can be found by contacting the respective National Forest offices or BLM field offices and the Incident Information System
website at inciweb.nwcg.gov.

Thank You Hunters and Recreational Shooters
Arizona’s rich outdoor heritage is enjoyed by all — thanks to hunters like you, whose purchase of hunting
and recreational shooting equipment supports wildlife management and habitat enhancement in the Grand
Canyon State. When you purchase a rifle, ammunition, archery equipment, and other sporting gear, you pay a
federal excise tax and import duties.
Since 1937, this money has been collected by the federal government and redistributed to the states using a
formula based on hunting license sales and the state’s land area. In 2018, that meant more than $22 million for
game management in Arizona. This money pays for game surveys, hunter education classes, wildlife water
catchment construction, wildlife research and shooting range development and operations, among other
projects. Hunters like you are part of the largest and most successful wildlife conservation programs in the
world. Thank you.

ARCHERY-ONLY DEER NONPERMIT-TAG SPECIAL REGULATION:
• Archery-only deer nonpermit-tags are valid for one calendar year. If you plan to hunt in January, you must purchase another archery-only
deer nonpermit-tag for the new year.
• Hunters are responsible for determining if their desired hunt unit is still open before hunting by checking the status online at
www.azgfd.gov/harvestreports
• When the number of deer harvested during the over-the-counter, nonpermit-tag archery season equals the harvest limit for a particular unit
and legal deer has been reached, that unit will close to archery deer hunting at sundown the Wednesday immediately following for the remain-
der of the archery deer hunting season.
• MANDATORY REPORTING (R12-4-308): All archery-only deer nonpermit-tag hunters must report their deer harvest online at
www.azgfd.gov/harvestreports within 48 hours of taking a deer. The report shall include the hunter’s name, hunting license number, tag num-
ber, species of deer taken, management unit where the deer was taken, and telephone number at which the hunter can be reached to obtain
additional information.

harvested with a restricted nonpermit-tag issued through this process.

Big Game — Turkey
For further information
on turkeys, their habitat,
range, natural history, or
where you can hunt them
in Arizona, please visit FLAGSTAFF
www.azgfd.gov.
PHOENIX
TUCSON
■ Distribution
LEGAL REQUIREMENTS
Any valid hunting or combination license EXCEPT a Short-term Combination Hunt and Fish License (See table on page 5 for
License Required
Resident and Non-resident license fees).
Hunt Permit-tag obtained only through application and draw process as prescribed in R12-4-104 and R12-4-114.
Tag Required EXCEPT for Youth-Only and Archery-Only turkey where a hunt nonpermit-tag must be obtained from a license dealer as
prescribed in R12-4-114.
Hunt Numbers Required When applying for big game hunt permit-tags, you must use hunt numbers.
Limited Weapon-Shotgun Shooting Shot Hunt: Any shotgun shooting shot as prescribed in R12-4-318 or crossbow or bow
and arrow as prescribed in R12-4-304. Archery-Only Hunt: Bow and arrow as prescribed in R12-4-304 and R12-4-318, or
Legal Methods of Take
crossbow if you possess a crossbow permit as allowed under R12-4-216. Centerfire rifles, muzzleloading rifles and handguns
are not legal methods of take.
Bag Limit One (1) turkey per calendar year.
Open Areas do not include areas within municipal parks, municipal preserves, county parks, county preserves, airports,
Open Areas golf courses, or posted water treatment facilities (except as specifically opened in this Commission Order) or areas closed
to hunting under A.R.S. Sections 17-303 and 17-304 or Commission Rules R12-4-321, R12-4-801, R12-4-802 and R12-4-803.
To hunt turkey in Arizona, you need a valid hunting or combination license and a turkey tag. To hunt turkey during a Limited Weapon-Shotgun Shooting Shot
season, you must apply through a draw for a hunt permit-tag. For Archery-Only hunts, you may purchase a nonpermit-tag from a license dealer as prescribed in
R12-4-114. For Youth-Only hunts, you may purchase a nonpermit-tag from a Department office or license dealer.
The Department offers “Youth-Only” designated hunts, in which persons are eligible to participate up to their 18th birthday. A youth hunter, whose 18th birthday
occurs after opening day of a “Youth-Only” designated hunt for which the hunter has a valid permit or tag, may continue to participate for the duration of the
“Youth-Only” designated hunt. Persons ages 10 through 13 must have satisfactorily completed a Hunter Education Course that is approved by the Director per
ARS 17-332(E). Hunter Education classes fill up quickly. If you need your Hunter Education card before your hunt, get ahead of the game and register now. To
register for a Hunter Education class, visit www.azgfd.gov/huntered or call 623-236-7239. No one under age 10 may hunt big game in Arizona. A youth must pay
the higher fee unless applying only for “Youth-Only” hunts.

LEGAL REQUIREMENTS
Any valid hunting or combination license EXCEPT a Short-term Combination Hunt and Fish License (See table on page 5 for
License Required
Resident and Non-resident license fees).
Tag Required Hunt Permit-tag obtained only through application and draw process as prescribed in R12-4-104 and R12-4-114.
Hunt Numbers Required When applying for big game hunt permit-tags, you must use hunt numbers.
Legal Methods of Take General Hunt: Any firearm or bow and arrow as prescribed in R12-4-304.
Legal Wildlife Definition Ram means any male bighorn sheep, excluding male lambs.
Bag Limit One (1) desert bighorn sheep in a lifetime. One (1) Rocky Mountain bighorn sheep in a lifetime.
Open Areas do not include areas within municipal parks, municipal preserves, county parks, county preserves,
airports, golf courses, or posted water treatment facilities (except as specifically opened in this Commission Order) or
Open Areas
areas closed to hunting under A.R.S. Sections 17-303 and 17-304 or Commission Rules R12-4-321, R12-4-801, R12-4-
802 and R12-4-803.
To hunt bighorn sheep in Arizona, you need a valid hunting or combination license and a bighorn sheep tag. To hunt bighorn sheep in any season, you must
apply for and obtain a hunt permit-tag through the draw.
BIGHORN SHEEP SPECIAL REGULATIONS:
• MANDATORY PHYSICAL INSPECTION (R12-4-308): A bighorn sheep hunter shall check out either in person or by designee within three days after the close of
the season. The hunter or designee shall submit the intact horns and skull for inspection and photographing. A Department representative shall affix a mark
or seal to one horn of each bighorn sheep lawfully taken under Commission Order. It is unlawful for any person to remove, alter, or obliterate the mark or seal.
Successful hunters should contact the Department office, where they plan to bring their harvested bighorn sheep, by telephone to coordinate inspections.

BISON SPECIAL REGULATIONS:

KAIBAB PLATEAU BISON HUNT SPECIAL NOTE: A significant portion of the original House Rock herd has moved to the Grand Canyon National Park where
hunting is not allowed. Hunters are advised that their ability to successfully harvest a bison may be significantly impacted. As with any hunt, circumstances
beyond the control of the Arizona Game and Fish Department may prevent the permit holder from being successful. The Arizona Game and Fish Department and
Commission disclaims any responsibility to reissue or replace a permit, to reinstate bonus points, to refund any fees, or to provide any other form of relief.
ATTENTION: Bison hunters are strongly encouraged to review bison hunting information at
www.azgfd.gov/bisonhunt and to watch the recorded bison hunting webinars.

■ Distribution
LEGAL REQUIREMENTS
Any valid hunting or combination license except a Short-term Combination Hunt and Fish License (See table on page 5 for
License Required
Resident and Non-resident license fees).
Tag Required Nonpermit-tag obtained from a license dealer as prescribed in R12-4-114.
General Hunt: Any firearm, pre-charged pneumatic weapon, crossbow, or bow and arrow as prescribed in R12-4-304. Archery
Legal Methods of Take Hunt: Bow and arrow as prescribed in R12-4-304 and R12-4-318, or crossbow if you possess a crossbow permit as prescribed
in R12-4-216.
Two (2) bears per calendar year, provided one (1) is taken with a restricted nonpermit-tag issued under R12-4-115 (Population
Bag Limit
Management Seasons). See page 49 for information about how to apply for a population management restricted nonpermit-tag.
Open Areas do not include areas within municipal parks, municipal preserves, county parks, county preserves,
airports, golf courses, or posted water treatment facilities (except as specifically opened in this Commission Order)
Open Areas
or areas closed to hunting under A.R.S. Sections 17-303 and 17-304 or Commission Rules R12-4-321, R12-4-801, R12-
4-802 and R12-4-803.
To hunt bear in Arizona, you need a valid hunting or combination license and a bear tag. To hunt bear in any season, you must obtain a nonpermit-tag from a
license dealer or any Arizona Game and Fish Department office.
BEAR SPECIAL REGULATIONS:
• Hunters are responsible for determining if their desired hunt unit is still open before hunting by checking the status online at
www.azgfd.gov/harvestreports.
• MANDATORY REPORTING (R12-4-308): All hunters must report their bear harvest online at www.azgfd.gov/harvestreports or in person within 48 hours of
taking a bear. The report shall include the hunter’s name, hunting license number, tag number, sex of the bear taken, management unit where the bear was
taken, and telephone number at which the hunter can be reached to obtain additional information.
• MANDATORY PHYSICAL INSPECTION (R12-4-308): Within 10 days of taking a bear, the hunter (or their designee) shall present the bear’s skull and hide
with attached proof of sex, accessible and identifiable, to a designated Arizona Game and Fish Department employee for inspection. If the skull and hide
are frozen, they should be defrosted prior to presenting for inspection as a premolar tooth will be removed from the bear. If the skull and hide are frozen,
we will not be able to complete the check-out and you will be asked to reschedule. Successful hunters must contact the Department office, where they
plan to bring their harvested bear, by telephone to coordinate inspections.
• In accordance with R12-4-305(H), an individual may retain the carcass of a bear taken under ARS 17-302 when the season is closed by attaching their bear
nonpermit-tag and following the harvest check in procedures.
BEAR SPECIAL NOTE: Hunters may encounter bears wearing radio collars across the state. The collared bears are part of several ongoing research projects.
If you harvest a collared bear, and it is OK to do so, please return the collar, as well as any other marking device, such as an ear tag, to a Department office or
officer when doing the required physical check-in of the carcass parts.

Bag Limit Change for Deer, Javelina and Bear (Population Management Seasons Only): In limited
cases, the department offers population management hunts under the provisions of R12-4-115
(page 92). These hunters are selected from the Supplemental Hunter Pool and randomly draw for
participation. You do not gain or lose bonus points when you apply for, or participate in, a population
management hunt, and this bag limit increase only applies for deer, javelina and bear harvested with
a permit issued through this random draw.

How to Identify a Mount Graham Red Squirrel
The Mount Graham red squirrel is a subspecies that can only In contrast, tassel-eared squirrels (Abert’s) on Mount Graham are
be found in the upper elevation conifer forests of the Pinaleño distinguished from red squirrels by their larger size, with adults
Mountains. The subspecies was thought to have been extinct usually 18-23 inches in total length and weighing an average of
in the 1950s, but was rediscovered decades later and granted 22 ounces. Abert’s squirrels are gray to black in color, with pale
protection under the Endangered Species Act in 1987. underparts and a rufous patch on the lower back. They are most
readily recognized by their prominent winter-tufted ears and long
The Mount Graham red squirrel is a grayish-brown tree squirrel,
(9-11 inches) bushy tails with gray above and white fur below.
tinged with shades of rust or yellow along the back. In summer, a
dark lateral line separates the light-colored underparts from the
grayer or browner sides. Red squirrel ears are slightly tufted in the
winter and the tail is bushy. On average, adult red squirrels are
about 13 inches in total length, including a tail that’s 5-6 inches
long, and they weigh about 8 ounces.

Quail hunters are reminded that A.R.S. 17-309 requires wildlife in possession be identifiable as to species. Commission Rule R12-4-305 states that for a person
transporting or possessing quail, that each quail have attached a fully feathered head, or a fully feathered wing, or a leg with foot attached.
Quail hunters in units 36A, 36B, and 36C are advised that endangered, Federally protected masked bobwhites may be present outside the borders of Buenos Aires
National Wildlife Refuge. Hunters are legally liable and responsible for knowing their target before shooting. Killing a masked bobwhite could result in license
revocation, hefty civil and Federal fines, and jail time. Know your target.

Small Game — Sandhill Crane
For further information
on sandhill cranes, their
habitat, range, natural
history, or where
FLAGSTAFF
you can hunt them in
Arizona, please visit
www.azgfd.gov.
PHOENIX
TUCSON
■ Distribution
LEGAL REQUIREMENTS
For hunters under age 10, you must either have a valid hunting or combination license or be accompanied by an adult who
License Required possesses a valid hunting or combination license. For hunters 10 years and older, any valid hunting or combination license
(See table on page 5 for Resident and Non-resident license fees).
Three (3) sandhill cranes per 3-day hunt in a season; possession is six (6) per calendar year, with no more than three (3) taken
Bag and Possession Limit in any 3-day hunt in a season.
Sandhill cranes may be taken only with a 10 gauge or smaller shotgun capable of holding not more than three (3) shells.
Legal Methods of Take
Sandhill cranes may also be taken by falconry, by bow and arrow, or crossbow.
No person may take sandhill cranes from any motor-driven vehicle, including aircraft. Special regulations govern
Motor-Vehicle Restrictions
handicapped hunters.
Sandhill cranes may be hunted from a watercraft (except a sinkbox) including those propelled by motor, sail and wind, or both
when the motor has been completely shut off and/or the sails furled and its progress therefrom has ceased. The watercraft
may be drifting as a result of current or wind action on the hull, beached or moored, resting at anchor, or may be propelled
Watercraft Restrictions
by paddle, oars or pole. A watercraft under power may be used to retrieve dead or crippled sandhill cranes but no shooting
is permitted while the watercraft is underway. It is illegal to drive or rally birds toward hunters by use of any motor-driven
conveyance or sail boat.
Sandhill cranes may not be taken with the aid of live decoys, recorded bird calls or electronically amplified bird calls. It is
Decoys, Calling and Baiting illegal to take birds over baited areas. An area is considered baited for 10 days after the bait has been removed, and a hunter
need not know an area has been baited to be in violation.
All sandhill cranes downed shall be retrieved if possible, and retained in the custody of the hunter in the field. Crippled birds
must be killed immediately. Any birds given to or left with another person must be tagged by the hunter with a tag showing his
Retrieval and Tagging
name, address and the number and species of birds, and the dates they were killed. Packages in which migratory game birds
are shipped must show the names of the sender and receiver, and the number and species of birds contained in the parcel.
No person may, during any one day take more than one daily bag limit or possess more than one daily bag limit in the field, or
Bag Limit while returning to the car, hunting camp or home. One feathered wing must be left attached to all migratory game birds until
they reach their final destination.
For information regarding the importation of migratory game birds killed in another country, hunters should consult federal
regulations (50CFR 20.61 through 20.66). One fully feathered wing must remain attached to all migratory game birds being
Importation transported between the port of entry and the hunter’s home or to a migratory bird preservation facility. No person shall
import any migratory game birds from any country except Canada unless such birds are dressed (except as required above),
drawn and the head and feet are removed. No person shall import migratory game birds belonging to another person.
No person may take sandhill cranes during closed season. Sandhill cranes may be taken only during hours open to hunting.
Seasons and Shooting Hours
Shooting hours: one-half hour before sunrise until sunset.
The Willcox Playa Wildlife Area (in Unit 30A) and Whitewater Draw Wildlife Area (in Unit 30B) are closed to all sandhill crane
Closed Areas
hunting. See notes for description of closed areas.
Open Areas do not include areas within municipal parks, municipal preserves, county parks, county preserves, airports, golf
Open Areas courses, or posted water treatment facilities (except as specifically opened in this Commission Order) or areas closed to
hunting under A.R.S. Sections 17-303 and 17-304 or Commission Rules R12-4-321, R12-4-801, R12-4-802 and R12-4-803.
Sportsmen should note that one feathered wing (or head for sandhill crane only) must be left attached to all sandhill cranes
One Feathered Wing
until they reach their final destination.
The Department offers “Youth-Only” designated hunts, in which persons are eligible to participate up to their 18th birthday. A youth hunter, whose 18th birthday
occurs after opening day of a “Youth-Only” designated hunt for which the hunter has a valid permit or tag, may continue to participate for the duration of the
“Youth-Only” designated hunt.
All General sandhill crane hunts have a mandatory check station of harvested sandhill cranes this year. Successfully drawn hunters will be sent additional information
by email on how to comply with this mandatory check of all harvested sandhill cranes.

NOTES ARE ON PAGES 65-74.
ATTENTION:
All General sandhill crane hunts have a mandatory check station of harvested sandhill cranes this year.
Successfully drawn hunters will be sent additional information by email on how to comply with this
mandatory check of all harvested sandhill cranes.
Almost all sandhill cranes are found on private lands in Arizona. This means that hunters must contact
landowners prior to hunts to obtain permission to hunt on their properties. The Arizona Game and Fish
Department will no longer be sending informational letters to individual hunters who draw sandhill crane
permits. Hunters should refer to the small game notes in this booklet and www.azgfd.gov/cranehunt for
information on sandhill crane hunts in Arizona, including history, tips and areas to hunt, hunter surveys and
mandatory hunter check stations.

Mexican Wolf Recovery
An important step in accomplishing recovery of the Mexican
wolf is to reduce inadvertent illegal shooting of this subspecies.
To date, many wolves have been shot, and it is probable that
some of these shootings were cases of mistaken identity, where
the shooter believed the target was a coyote.
It is important for hunters to distinguish wolves from coyotes,
especially when the sighting is brief, the animal is far away,
the wolf is a juvenile, or the wolf is in its summer coat. Hunters
should exercise extra caution from July to November because
wolf pups are active, and their appearance and behavior
can make them appear like coyotes. You are responsible for
identifying your target before shooting.
If you are hunting in Units 1, 2A, 2B, 2C, 3A, 3B, 3C, 4A, 4B, 5A,
5B, 6A, 6B, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11M, 22N, 22S, 23N, 23S, 27, 29, 30A, or near
the United States/Mexico border, please be aware that Mexican
wolves may be present. Mexican wolves are federally protected
under the Endangered Species Act. Killing one can result in
criminal penalties of up to $50,000, and/or not more than one
year in jail, and/or a civil penalty of up to $25,000. The unlawful
A wolf’s tail is long (almost to the ground) and straight. The nose and face
killing of a wolf is a violation of A.R.S. 17-309.A.10 and could
have a blocky appearance. Not all wolves are collared. See page 55 for infor-
result in a minimum civil assessment of $8,000 (ARS 17-314),
mation on how to identify a Mexican wolf from a coyote.
loss of hunting privileges for up to five years (ARS 17-340) and
additional criminal fines as determined by the court. A person For more information on Mexican wolves, visit www.azgfd.gov/
may harass, injure or kill a wolf in defense of human life, but the wolf or www.fws.gov/southwest/es/mexicanwolf. Call the U.S.
action must be reported within 24 hours. Fish and Wildlife Service’s Alpine office at 928-339-4329 or the Ar-
izona Game and Fish Department’s Pinetop office at 928-532-2391.
Fair Chase
Fair chase is the ethical, sportsmanlike and lawful pursuit and
taking of free-range wildlife in a manner that does not give a
hunter or an angler improper or unfair advantage over such
wildlife.
Fair chase has been embraced as the proper conduct of a
sportsman/sportswoman in the field and has been taught to new
hunters for more than a century. It pays respect to the traditions
of hunting and angling by emphasizing the development of an
individual’s skills rather than reliance on practices or technologies
that overwhelm the quarry’s ability to elude detection or take.
In many situations, fair chase is not something that is enforceable
by law. Rather it should be guided by each person’s ethical
compass, which compels them to do the right thing when no one
else is watching — even when doing the wrong thing is legal.
The support of fair chase and respect for the traditions of hunting
and angling are every sportsman’s responsibility.
So remember, Hunt Hard, Hunt Fair.
Learn more: www.azgfd.gov/fairchase

Operation Game Thief
Poachers are a serious threat to Arizona’s wildlife.
Every animal a poacher kills deprives ethical sportsmen and wildlife enthusiasts of wildlife-related recreational opportunities. Report any
wildlife violations to the Operation Game Thief hotline: 800-352-0700. The hotline operates 7 days a week, 24 hours a day, and the phone
number is printed on hunting and fishing licenses, so it’s always with you in the field. All calls will remain confidential. Rewards of up to
$20,000 may be offered upon arrest in certain cases.
A majority of cases are initiated by concerned sportsmen who call the Operation Game Thief hotline. You can and do make a difference!
Poachers are thieves stealing from you. If you witness a violation the following information will assist us during our investigation:

is unlawful.

Make the Switch to Lead-free Ammunition
Voluntarily choosing to use lead-free ammunition while hunting
is good for the conservation of wildlife and ecosystem health.
Lead bullet fragments remaining in animal parts left in the field
can cause lead poisoning in scavengers. You can help prevent this
by making the switch to lead-free ammunition when hunting.
Lead and Wildlife
• Lead fragments left in carcasses and gut piles can be ingested
by scavenging wildlife. These lead sources can cause illness or
death in individual animals.
• Birds are the most susceptible to lead toxicity and population-lev-
el effects have been documented California condors.
• Lead-free ammunition is becoming more widely available and
reasonably priced. Using lead-free alternatives can prevent lead
poisoning of wildlife.
• Hunters and anglers have been the primary supporters of wild-
life conservation in North America since the early 1900s.
Lead-free Ammo Facts
• As lead bullets mushroom after impact, they break into frag-
ments. These lead fragments are left behind in the carcass.
• Most lead-free bullets are designed to peel open and remain intact.
This requires less energy resulting in greater impact at reason-
able ranges and deeper penetration.

• Developed as a premium bullet for big game hunting in Afri-
ca, copper bullets gained a reputation as being very accurate.
• Lead-free bullets are considered premium bullets and are
similar in price to premium lead-based bullets . While cheap-
er lead-based options exist, they do not deliver the same
performance.

Get Ahead of the Game Course Information
The purpose of the Hunter Education program is to promote safety and At a minimum your Hunter Education Course will
develop responsible, knowledgeable, and involved hunters. The Hunter include the following topics:
Education program strives to create hunters who understand the importance
• Responsibility, safety, skills
of complying with hunting laws and behaving ethically.
• Funding sources
Hunter Education is only required for youth under the age of 14 to hunt big
• Conservation, fair chase, fair share, hunters’ ethics and
game in Arizona. If you have a Hunter Education certification from another
hunters’ image
state, your certification meets Arizona Hunter Education requirements.
In order to qualify for the hunter education bonus point or conservation • Planning and preparation, maps and compasses,
education bonus point, you must take an Arizona Hunter Education course or survival skills, coping with extreme weather and
Ethically Hunting Arizona. Only one permanent bonus point can be awarded. basic first aid
Youth under 14 hunting big game in Arizona must carry their Hunter • Firearm safety, nomenclature, function, handling and
Education card and valid hunting license while in the field. Youth under 14 shot-selection
can enter the draw without having Hunter Education, but must complete • Wildlife conservation, management and identification
Hunter Education prior to their hunt. In order to apply for a big game permit a
• Marksmanship, rifle and shotgun shooting, hunting strat-
person must be at least 10 years of age by the time their hunt starts and have
egies, vital shots and care of game
successfully completed hunter education. Youth can purchase a hunting
license without having attended Hunter Education. Any individual 9 years • Arizona hunting laws, regulations and licensing
of age or older may complete a Hunter Education course offered through the
Arizona Game and Fish Department.
Note: The online course is a fully virtual option and is for Arizona residents only.
Trapper Education
Pursuant to A.R.S. 17-333.02 Any person
born on or after Jan. 1, 1967, is required to
successfully complete a Trapper Education

Commission-owned Shooting Ranges
Shooting facilities provide a safe environment to practice for upcoming hunting seasons. Shooting
ranges provide shooters with many benefits, including controlled settings, training and education.
At a range, shooters at any skill level can practice, sight-in firearms, receive instruction and discover
communities of like-minded enthusiasts.
